# T0-46: Interview Loops for GenAI Roles: by Employer and Track

Source numbers such as [S3] point to rows of the Sources & Evidence Ledger near the end of this file; they are numbered in the order of first citation. Grades follow EGP-1.0: A is a primary page that was opened (an employer's or vendor's own statement about its own offering); B is a platform dataset or reputable reporting; C is a third-party, vendor or practitioner report, always attributed; D is a single anecdote or an unverified statement; E marks something searched for and not found (absences only); M is my own arithmetic; P is practice guidance that nobody has measured. Ledger modifiers are COI (the source sells or is the thing described), stale (published before 2026-01-01), single-source and undated. Research ran on 2026-11-13 and 2026-11-14 (the retrieval dates in the ledger); the report was assembled on 2026-11-17. The brief's anchor date is 2026-11-11 and its evidence window runs from 2025-07-01 to 2026-11-11. The published dates of the cited sources span 2020-11-13 to 2026-11-12: sources before 2025-07-01 are background only and are flagged, and one prep-vendor page dated 2026-11-12 post-dates the anchor and is used only for report counts. Dates in tables are the publication date of the newest source behind a row; where a page shows no date, the row carries the retrieval date and the ledger marks that source undated. Pages were opened with a fetch tool that returns a summary of the page text, so load-bearing figures should be re-checked against the pages before they are reused.

## Prior

Recorded on 2026-11-13, before any search. All rows are grade D (memory).

| # | Proposition | Confidence | Grade |
|---|---|---|---|
| P1 | Anthropic's live interview rounds ban AI assistance by default and its public candidate guidance says so, while some application stages (applications, take-homes) may allow AI help with disclosure. | 70% | D |
| P2 | Meta runs an AI-enabled coding round (CoderPad with a built-in assistant) in 2026 loops for some engineering roles, replacing one classic coding round. | 65% | D |
| P3 | Google reinstated in-person rounds for some engineering roles since mid-2025, with AI-assisted cheating as a stated reason. | 70% | D |
| P4 | Take-homes are uncommon in GenAI loops at Big Tech and frontier labs but common at AI-native startups; Anthropic has used a take-home for performance-engineering candidates. | 60% | D |
| P5 | No employer publishes stage-by-stage pass rates; the best public onsite-to-offer aggregates sit around 20-30%. | 70% | D |
| P6 | Anthropic, OpenAI and Google each list per-million-token prices on their own pages; as of late 2026 flagship input prices sit between 1 and 15 USD and output prices between 5 and 75 USD per million tokens. | 50% | D |

## Research Question

**Decision question (D-009).** As of 2026-11-11, what do interview loops for GenAI roles look like by employer and by interview track: which rounds, what each assesses, the policy on AI assistants, take-homes, in-person rounds, timelines and pass rates, so that a job seeker can classify a loop and prepare for the right one? The door is two-way: employer guides or AI-use policies, consistent reports of new round types, or published pass-rate data would change the advice.

**Sub-questions.**

1. Which rounds and formats does each employer use for each track it hires for?
2. What do employers allow or forbid on candidates' use of AI in applications, take-homes and live rounds?
3. Which employers reinstated in-person rounds or added identity checks since 2025, and for what stated reason?
4. How common are take-homes in GenAI loops, how long are they, are they paid, and what is the AI rule?
5. Which GenAI system-design archetypes appear in 2025-2026 loops, and which model prices and limits do estimates need?
6. What do behavioural, values and research rounds assess, and how are they scored?
7. What do loops look like at GCCs, product companies and IT-services firms in India?
8. What are the timelines and any pass or offer rates by stage?
9. Which question themes are reported for each track (themes only, never model answers)?
10. Which of the 24 claims K1-K24 survive testing, including the two source conflicts inside K3 (Google) and K4 (Meta's AI-enabled round)?

**Scope as applied.**

- An employer is a hiring organisation; Amazon and its Indian arm (ADCI) are counted once. Each loop is classified into one of the brief's eight track IDs; an employer that hires for several tracks has one record per track with evidence.
- The evidence window is 2025-07-01 to 2026-11-11. Reports before 2026-01-01 carry the stale modifier. AI screening and AI interviewers at the application stage are out of scope, as are model answers.
- Model prices come from vendor pricing pages only. Where a page shows no date, the table gives the read date.
- No login, paywall or bot wall was bypassed. A Blind thread on Anthropic's staff loop was recorded as Blocked and is not used [S1].
- The brief's references to the knowledge base are not used as evidence.

**Method.** The report rests on 104 ledger sources from 50 publishers. Searches used a web-search tool that returns titles and URLs; pages were opened with a fetch tool. The data appendix holds 31 loop records for 25 employers (Anthropic, Google DeepMind, OpenAI, xAI, Amazon, Google, Meta, Microsoft, NVIDIA, Canva, Cursor, Databricks, Perplexity, Scale AI, Goldman Sachs, JPMorgan Chase, Deloitte, McKinsey, Accenture, Capgemini, Cognizant, HCLTech, Infosys, Iris Software, Wipro), 90 prompt records, 17 practice records, 679 measure records (mostly model-price rows) and 28 absence records. Prompt records give the question theme as reported; their `covers` and `red_flags` fields are practice guidance (grade P) and are not taken from the reports.

Research moves and documented falsification searches:

- CORROBORATE. Each round format was sought in at least two independent reports. Meta's AI-assisted coding round is corroborated by three publishers (CoderPad's own blog, interviewing.io and PracHub reports) [S2, S3, S4]. Most other loop rows rest on one prep-vendor guide, and the ledger marks them single-source.
- FALSIFY, uniform loops. The assumption that an employer runs one loop was tested and failed: Meta's loop differs by level [S5], Databricks differs between the AI Engineer guide and a forward-deployed-engineer report [S6, S7], Google's fifth round is optional and domain-specific [S8], and Cursor's final stage is described three different ways [S9, S10, S11].
- FALSIFY, K14. The IGotAnOffer Anthropic system-design page was opened to look for the two prompts K14 names (a model evaluation and monitoring system; a prompt caching and retrieval system). It lists other themes, so K14 is contradicted for that page [S12]. Other IGotAnOffer pages were not opened.
- FALSIFY, K15 and K5. A search for AceOffer's tally of 112 Anthropic design reports returned PracHub, Interview Query and 1point3acres pages but no AceOffer page. A search for a latency-debugging exercise at Anthropic found no report. Both are recorded as absences.
- FALSIFY, K21 and K9. The Trustpilot page for Final Round AI shows different figures from K21 [S13], and Hello Interview's Premium course list shows no ML System Design course, which K9 assumes [S14].
- FALSIFY, pass rates. Reports from Gem, Ashby, Greenhouse and Karat were read for any employer-published stage rate; none exists, and the only per-employer offer rates are prep-vendor figures from 5 to 9 reports [S6, S15, S16, S17, S18].
- FALSIFY, governance. A search for governance-track interview sources returned only job-aggregator pages, so the track has no loop evidence (see Absences).
- WIDEN. India (ADCI, Infosys, Wipro, TCS, HCLTech, Cognizant, Accenture, Capgemini, Deloitte, Iris Software), consultancies (McKinsey QuantumBlack, Deloitte) and banks (Goldman Sachs, JPMorgan Chase).

## Key Findings

| # | Finding | Key evidence | Grade |
|---|---|---|---|
| KF1 | AI-assistant rules are set per employer and per stage, not per sector. Anthropic allows AI to refine a candidate-written application and to prepare, and bans it in live rounds and take-homes unless the instructions say otherwise; Canva requires AI in one round; Meta and Google run assistant-enabled rounds alongside AI-free ones. | Anthropic's guidance page (updated 2025-07-10, COI) [S19]; Anthropic's engineering post of 2026-01-21 says its performance-engineering take-home allowed AI tools (COI) [S20]; Canva's post of 2025-06-11 [S21]; Meta's built-in assistant in CoderPad [S2, S3]; Google's Gemini-assisted pilot reported by Business Insider via Yahoo Finance, 2026-05-14 [S22]. | A/C |
| KF2 | Where AI is allowed, rounds grade direction, review and ownership, not prompting. Canva lists task breakdown, finding faults in generated code and production standard, and calls prompting and accepting output the weakest approach. Meta's round is described as judging critical review, testing and communication. Scale AI wanted the candidate's own prompt; Accenture wanted every generated line explained. | [S21]; interviewing.io on Meta [S3]; one Microsoft CoreAI report of five fixes in a multi-file repository in about 45 minutes with an AI agent [S23]; a Scale AI report [S24]; Accenture guide [S25]. | A/C |
| KF3 | K4's coding-or-design conflict at Meta resolves into two different rounds: a 60-minute AI-assisted coding round and, in 2026 reports, a separate AI-enabled or agentic design round. The pilot is dated October 2025, earlier than the spring 2026 signal in K4. In one report the design round offered no AI despite prep materials. | CoderPad's blog of 2025-10-30 [S2]; interviewing.io [S3]; two PracHub reports of March and April 2026 interviews [S4, S26]; level differences [S5]. | C |
| KF4 | Anthropic's infrastructure loop centres on inference-serving design, with a design-document review reported twice (2026-04-16 and 2026-09-18, both on PracHub) and a performance-engineering take-home that Anthropic says it cut from 4 hours (version 1, from early 2024) to 2 hours (version 2, mid-2025). No latency-debugging report was found. | Anthropic's own post, COI [S20]; PracHub [S27, S28]; prep-vendor list of serving themes dated 2026-06-09 [S12]. | A/C |
| KF5 | Take-homes are a minority format overall (about 13% of hires in Ashby's all-role data) and concentrate in senior, research and AI-native roles: 4-8 hours at Anthropic (research engineering), Cursor and xAI, 4-6 hours at Scale AI and Perplexity. Only Cursor's Project Day is described as paid. None was reported for the Big Tech or Indian IT-services loops read. | [S16]; guides [S10, S29, S30, S31, S32]; paid work trial [S9]; OpenAI says take-homes are team-specific [S33]. | B/C |
| KF6 | In-person rounds returned at Google (announced June 2025) and McKinsey, with curbing AI use in virtual interviews as the stated reason; OpenAI finals are virtual by default with an optional San Francisco onsite. No employer-published identity-check rule was found; a vendor survey puts candidate-admitted interview fraud at 6% (n=3,000, 2Q25). | Entrepreneur, 2025-08-18 [S34]; OpenAI guide [S33]; Gartner release of 2025-07-31 (COI, stale) [S35]. | B/C |
| KF7 | Onsite-to-offer for engineering is 26% in Gem's 2024 data, not 29%; interview-to-offer is 14% at enterprises and 22% at startups, and Ashby's onsite pass-through is about 24%. No employer publishes its own stage rate. Prep-vendor offer rates (20% to 80%) rest on 5 to 9 reports and are not usable as odds. | [S15, S16, S36]; Dataford guides [S6, S25, S37, S38]. | B |
| KF8 | Entry binds harder than passing. Gem's 2024 data show 6% of inbound applicants reaching pre-onsite against 44% for sourced candidates, and 0.5% of applications ending in a hire; Greenhouse reports 254 applicants per open role. | [S15, S17, S36]; referred candidates screen at 52% against 35% [S16]. The advice to run 3-5 staggered loops has no outcome evidence (P). | B/P |
| KF9 | Guides give 2-5 weeks from first screen to decision for IT-services firms, banks and consultancies, 6-10 weeks for Google and DeepMind, and 1-2 weeks for xAI senior candidates. Ashby's first-to-last interview span is 14.4 to 17.9 days. Guides conflict on Microsoft (3-5 against 4-6 weeks) and NVIDIA (1-2 against 6-8 weeks). | [S8, S25, S32, S38, S39, S40, S41, S42]; [S16]; conflicts [S43, S44, S45]. | B/C |
| KF10 | Vendor pages give per-million-token prices for Anthropic, OpenAI, Google, xAI, Mistral and DeepSeek. Among the models tabled, input runs from 0.10 USD (Claude Haiku 5.5, gpt-6-luna, gemini-2.5-flash-lite) to 10 USD (gpt-6-astra), and output from 0.40 USD (gemini-2.5-flash-lite) to 50 USD (gpt-6-astra); the legacy Claude Opus 4.1 still lists 15 and 75. Context windows are shown only on some pages. | See the model-price table; Anthropic rows are COI [S46]; OpenAI [S47]; Google [S48]. | A |
| KF11 | GenAI design archetypes in 2025-2026 reports: retrieval with per-user access control, agent loop control and tool routing, evaluation gates, inference serving (batching, admission control, fairness), a low-latency completion service, tool-integration protocols and ticket-triage or code-review agents. | [S4, S6, S7, S10, S25, S26, S27, S28, S44]; a prep vendor's three families (predictive, generative, agentic) [S49]. | C |
| KF12 | Behavioural and values rounds can decide the outcome but the rules differ. Anthropic runs a values and safety round that one report calls the top late-stage gate; one Amazon guide says the Bar Raiser's No Hire veto is final; one Meta source says a behavioural round alone can downlevel an E5; Google's committee can downlevel and weighs variance heavily. | [S29, S50]; [S51]; [S5]; [S8]. | C |
| KF13 | India loops are shorter and more virtual: Amazon India (ADCI) mixes data structures, low-level design and GenAI coding; Infosys technical rounds last 15 minutes to an hour; Wipro, Accenture, HCLTech and Cognizant take 2-5 weeks. TCS publishes four AI hiring tracks but no round detail was found. | [S52, S53]; [S54, S55]; [S25, S39, S40, S56]; [S57]. | C/D |
| KF14 | Research loops combine a depth round, hand-written core ML code and research-problem scoping: DeepMind's coding round is 60 minutes without libraries, and OpenAI's finals run 4-6 hours with a discussion of the candidate's area. | [S42]; [S33]; Anthropic research-engineering stages [S29]; xAI [S32]. | A/C |
| KF15 | No prep platform has independent outcome evidence. Hello Interview's own page says its human mock programme ended on 2026-05-31; Final Round AI shows 2.8/5 on 280 Trustpilot reviews and a privacy policy that allows recordings to be used for AI training, which contradicts K21's figures. | [S58, S59]; [S13, S60]; outcome-evidence search [S61, S62, S63]. | A/B/E |

## Recommendation

For a job seeker preparing for GenAI loops as of 2026-11-11. Each rule names the option it recommends, the failure mode of that option and the evidence. Most evidence on loop contents is grade C (prep-vendor guides and candidate reports); the AI-use rules for Anthropic and Canva, the model prices and the vendor statements about their own products are grade A.

| # | Decision rule | Failure mode of this option | Evidence |
|---|---|---|---|
| R1 | Classify the loop before preparing. Ask the recruiter, and keep the reply in writing, for the rounds, the kind of design round (classic ML, application or infrastructure), the AI-tool rule for each round, any in-person requirement and the expected timeline. | A generic recruiter answer, or a loop that changes between stages: Meta differs by level, Cursor's final stage is described three ways, and Databricks differs by track. Re-ask before each stage. | KF1, KF3; [S5, S6, S7, S8, S9, S10, S11, S33] |
| R2 | Treat the AI rule as a per-round rule. Default to no AI in live rounds and in take-homes unless the written instructions allow it; use AI on application materials only where the employer's page allows it (Anthropic's does, for refining a draft you wrote yourself). | Importing one employer's rule into another, or relying on a dated page: Anthropic's guidance page is dated 2025-07-10 and its own post of 2026-01-21 shows a take-home where AI was allowed. An undeclared assistant in a no-AI round can end the process. | KF1; [S19, S20, S21] |
| R3 | In a round that provides an assistant, work as director and reviewer: split the task, give the assistant bounded sub-tasks, read and test what it returns, and say why you accept or reject each piece. Practise on a multi-file repository, not on single functions. | Treating the round as a prompting contest (Canva calls prompting and accepting output the weakest approach), or ignoring an assistant the round expects you to use. | KF2; [S3, S21, S23, S24, S25] |
| R4 | Keep classic algorithm and write-it-yourself coding practice for the AI-free rounds: Anthropic's live Python coding, Meta's classic rounds, DeepMind's 60-minute coding round and Amazon's agentic-AI loop, where one guide advises against leaning on high-level libraries. | Spending most of the practice time on AI-enabled formats, which the sources show as one round among several, not a replacement. | KF2, KF14; [S3, S29, S41, S42] |
| R5 | Prepare design rounds by track. Infrastructure: inference serving, batching, admission control and critiquing a flawed design document. Application: retrieval with access control, agent loop control, evaluation gates and tool integration. Classic ML design: pipelines, monitoring, and where an LLM would help. Keep the model-price table and the KV-cache formula (M) ready for estimates. | Prices and model names change within a quarter (freshness class V2) and some pages show no date; recheck the vendor page before quoting a number, and treat tabled figures as estimates, not as quotes. | KF4, KF10, KF11; [S6, S25, S27, S28, S44, S46, S47, S48] |
| R6 | At senior, research and AI-native roles, budget 4-8 hours for a take-home and ask three things before starting: the time cap, the AI rule and whether it is paid. | Unpaid multi-day work: only Cursor's Project Day is described as paid, and that rests on one guide (grade C). Anthropic's take-home reports differ by role. | KF5; [S9, S10, S16, S29, S30, S31, S32] |
| R7 | Assume at least one in-person round at Google and a face-to-face meeting at McKinsey; confirm for every other employer. | Assuming a virtual loop because OpenAI's finals are virtual by default; travel and visa lead times are not covered by the evidence read. | KF6; [S33, S34, S37, S43, S56] |
| R8 | Put effort into entry, and run several loops in the same track at once: sourced and referred routes convert far better than inbound applications. | Preparation spread across too many tracks, and offers that do not overlap because elapsed times range from 1 to 10 weeks. The advice to run 3-5 staggered loops has no outcome evidence (grade P). | KF8, KF9; [S15, S16, S17, S23, S36] |
| R9 | Read pass rates as a range, 14% to 26% for interview-stage conversion in Gem (engineering) and Ashby (all roles) data, and never as the odds at one employer. | False precision: the 29% in circulation is a mislabelled 2021 figure, and vendor offer rates from 5 to 9 reports swing from 20% to 80%. | KF7; [S6, S15, S16, S25, S36, S38] |
| R10 | Choose practice tools by what can be verified: Excalidraw (free) for design practice, after asking which board the interviewer uses; Hello Interview with care (AI-run practice only, human mocks ended on 2026-05-31). Avoid live answer-suggesting copilots in any round whose employer bans AI. | No independent outcome evidence exists for any platform, so every quality claim is the vendor's own (COI). Final Round AI's privacy policy allows recordings to be used for AI training and its Trustpilot score is 2.8/5 on 280 reviews. | KF15; [S13, S19, S58, S59, S60, S61, S62, S63, S64] |
| R11 | For India, prepare a data-structures, low-level-design and GenAI-coding mix for Amazon India-style loops, and project deep dives, GenAI fundamentals and managerial rounds for IT-services firms; expect 2 to 5 weeks, mostly virtual. | The evidence is prep-vendor and candidate reports (grades C and D) and no Indian AI lab, GCC-specific or aptitude-vendor page was found. | KF13; [S25, S39, S40, S52, S53, S54, S55, S56] |

**What would change these rules.** An employer publishing a guide or an AI-use policy (OpenAI's guide page states no candidate AI rule); consistent reports of a new round type, such as a latency-debugging exercise at Anthropic, which has one report in the brief and none found here; published stage pass rates; an independent outcome study of a prep platform; and any price change on the vendor pages, which should be re-read before every estimate.

## Alternatives Considered

| Option | Verdict | Reason | Evidence |
|---|---|---|---|
| Prepare one generic GenAI loop | Rejected | Loops differ by level, team and track within one employer; preparing for the wrong track is the failure the classification step prevents (K1 confirmed). | [S5, S6, S7, S8] |
| Prepare by employer type (lab, Big Tech, startup, services) instead of by track | Used as a starting prior only | Type predicts the round mix loosely (labs add values and research rounds; services firms add managerial rounds), but Meta, Databricks and Cursor each vary inside one employer. | [S9, S29, S54] |
| Rely on employers' own guides alone | Preferred where they exist, but not enough | Only Anthropic, OpenAI and Canva published pages in the evidence read, and OpenAI's page states no AI rule; everything else is third-party. | [S19, S21, S33] |
| Use an AI assistant in every round | Rejected | Anthropic's default is no AI in live rounds and take-homes; undeclared use risks the process, and AI-free rounds persist. | [S3, S19] |
| Drop classic coding practice and train only on AI-enabled formats | Rejected | Classic rounds remain AI-free at Meta and Anthropic, and AI-enabled rounds sit beside them. | [S3, S5, S29] |
| Read prep-vendor offer rates as employer odds | Rejected | The rates come from 5 to 9 self-selected reports. | [S6, S25, S37, S38] |
| Wait for employers to publish pass rates | Rejected | None was found; platform datasets already give usable ranges. | [S15, S16, S36] |
| Pay for a prep platform on its own outcome claims | Rejected | No independent outcome evidence was found (K8); the vendors' claims are COI. | [S59, S61, S62, S63] |
| Run loops strictly one after another | Rejected for the same track | Entry rates are low (0.5% of applications end in a hire), and sequential loops lose the chance to overlap offers; parallel loops in different tracks dilute preparation, so run several in one track. | [S15, S36] |

## Detailed Findings

### 1. Employer loops

One row per employer and track. "Rounds and elapsed time" is the duration field of the record plus the number of stages listed in the data appendix, where every stage is spelled out. "Date" is the publication date of the newest source behind the row; OpenAI's rows carry the retrieval date, 2026-11-13, because its guide page shows no date, and Infosys's row carries 2026-11-14 because its prep-vendor guide shows no date (a 2026-03 candidate report sits beside it). Grade C rows are prep-vendor guides or single candidate reports and describe the loop as reported, not as the employer confirms it. Rows for Anthropic that rest on Anthropic's own statements about itself are COI. The employers covered are: Anthropic, Google DeepMind, OpenAI, xAI, Amazon, Google, Meta, Microsoft, NVIDIA, Canva, Cursor, Databricks, Perplexity, Scale AI, Goldman Sachs, JPMorgan Chase, Deloitte, McKinsey, Accenture, Capgemini, Cognizant, HCLTech, Infosys, Iris Software, Wipro.

Source conflicts kept, with ranges and reasons (protocol rule 4):

- Anthropic system-design session length: one prep vendor gives 50-55 minutes in its overview but 35-60 and about 45 elsewhere on the same page, and another guide says 60 [S12, S29]. The page is internally inconsistent, so no single figure is given.
- Microsoft elapsed time: about 3 weeks in one CoreAI report, 3-5 weeks in the guide body and 4-6 weeks in the same guide's FAQ [S23, S43]. Range: 3-6 weeks.
- Databricks: 4-6 weeks in the AI Engineer guide, about 1.5 months and six rounds in one forward-deployed-engineer report of interviews from January to March 2026 [S6, S7]. The guide mentions no forward-deployed track and no customer round, so the AI-FDE loop is only partly confirmed.
- Cursor's work stage: a 4-8 hour take-home, a paid 8-9 hour Project Day that the guide says is not a take-home, and a two-day onsite on a real project (a 2025-10-28 report, flagged stale). The sources may describe one stage or differ by role; not resolved [S9, S10, S11].
- NVIDIA: an offer within 1-2 weeks after the loop (2026 guide) against 6-8 weeks end to end (2024 conversations, stale); the two figures measure different spans [S44, S45].
- Amazon: the agentic AI engineer guide lists the Bar Raiser both inside the loop and as a separate round [S41].

Candidate-side measures (vendor survey, COI, stale): 39% of candidates said they used AI during the application process (n=3,290, fieldwork 4Q24) and 6% admitted interview fraud, meaning posing as someone else or having someone else take part (n=3,000, 2Q25); the release is dated 2025-07-31 [S35]. Fortune reported on 2025-07-21 that Anthropic relaxed an earlier blanket ban on AI in applications; the start date of that ban is not established by the sources read [S74].

Integrity measures come from one vendor survey (COI, stale): 6% of candidates admitted interview fraud (n=3,000, 2Q25), and the same release forecasts that one in four candidate profiles worldwide will be fake by 2028, which is a prediction and not an observed rate [S35]. Anthropic's guidance bars AI help in live interviews unless the interviewer says otherwise, so a live copilot is a rule breach there, not a grey area [S19].

### 5. Take-homes

Prevalence, length, payment, AI rule and evaluation criteria. Across all roles, about 13% of hires in Ashby's customer data included a take-home (grade B) [S16]. None was reported in the Big Tech or Indian IT-services loops read. McKinsey's 70-120 minute online coding assessment is a timed test, not a take-home [S68]. ### 6. GenAI system-design archetypes

Archetypes that recur in 2025-2026 reports, with the themes a strong answer covers and the red flags. The reported-at column and the grade come from the reports (grade C); the covers and red-flag columns are practice guidance (grade P) written for this report, not taken from the sources, and they are themes only, never model answers. Estimation inputs for these rounds are in section 7: model prices, rate limits, GPU memory and the KV-cache formula.

| Archetype | Reported at | Themes a strong answer covers (P) | Red flags (P) | Sources |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Retrieval with access control | Databricks, HCLTech | Permissions at document and chunk level at query time; streaming ingestion and re-indexing; freshness and deletion; audit logging; text-to-SQL safety; tests for permission leaks. | Filtering after generation; batch-only re-indexing; unrestricted database access; no leak testing. | [S6, S7, S39] |
| Agent loop control | Meta (ticket triage and code review), Cursor (multi-file edits), Deloitte | Input and output contract; tool and context limits; confidence thresholds and human escalation; verification with tests; rollback; loop and cost limits; evaluation of routing. | Components with no control loop; unbounded loops; no verification or escalation path. | [S4, S10, S26, S67] |
| Tool-integration protocols | Accenture | Tool schemas and permission scoping; untrusted-content handling; confirmation for risky actions; logging and replay; attack testing. | Trusting tool output as instructions; one shared credential for all tools. | [S25] |
| Inference serving and APIs (multi-tenant) | Anthropic, Scale AI, NVIDIA, Cursor | Request queues and batching; fairness, quotas and per-client limits; capacity estimates; retries and idempotency; memory and cache budget; parallelism; latency budget; usage accounting. | Designing the model, not the serving system; no capacity estimate; ignoring backpressure; no memory arithmetic. | [S10, S12, S24, S44, S72] |
| Design-document review | Anthropic | Order findings by severity; separate requirement gaps from failures; check batch-latency and QPS arithmetic; scheduling a large and a small model on shared GPUs; retries; migration and validation plan. | Style issues before correctness flaws; no arithmetic; reading top to bottom without ranking. | [S27, S28] |
| Answer-engine retrieval and quality | Perplexity | Retrieval and reranking stages; latency and cost budget; caching; citation integrity; separating retrieval errors from generation errors; offline relevance sets and online comparison. | Tuning only the prompt; no latency or cost numbers; unverified citations. | [S31, S66] |
| Evaluation architecture | Databricks, Scale AI, DeepMind | Definitions of faithfulness and groundedness; rubric and examples; judge agreement with human labels and bias checks; test-set design, thresholds and regression detection; reproducible harnesses. | A judge never checked against humans; a gate on one score; no update path for the test set. | [S6, S30, S42, S73] |
| Production monitoring of LLM services | Microsoft, Cognizant, Goldman Sachs | Quality, latency and cost signals; trace sampling; alerting and rollback; drift tracking. | Monitoring uptime only; no rollback plan. | [S40, S43, S70] |
| Multi-agent orchestration and its evaluation | Databricks | Roles and handoffs; shared state and failures; end-to-end task success; trace-level debugging; cost and latency limits. | Evaluating agents only in isolation; no handoff contract. | [S6] |
| Regulated or client-scoped GenAI | Cognizant, McKinsey | Data handling and residency; access control and logging; drift and retraining approval; risk sign-off; splitting work between a classical model and an LLM. | Ignoring regulation; using an LLM where a simple model would do. | [S38, S40] |

A prep vendor groups AI design prompts into predictive, generative and agentic families; that grouping is the vendor's own framing and is COI [S49]. A separate vendor publishes a generative AI system-design course with 11 chapters; its page shows no price or date [S77].

### 8. Behavioural and values rounds, level calibration and committees

| Employer | What is assessed | Scoring and consequence | Grade and sources |
|---|---|---|---|
| Anthropic | A 200-400 word "why Anthropic" essay in the application; a values and safety discussion round (for example, a concrete AI safety problem); project ownership and conflict. | One report calls the culture interview the top late-stage hiring gate (a secondary summary of a Bloomberg Businessweek feature, 2026-05-29); team matching depends on open headcount. | C. [S29, S50, S65] |
| Amazon | Leadership Principles stories, a Bar Raiser from outside the hiring team, and a graded question on how the candidate uses AI tools. | Debrief with Hire or No-Hire votes backed by Leadership Principle evidence; one guide says the Bar Raiser's No-Hire veto is final and mixed votes can lead to a lower-level offer. | C. [S41, S51] |
| Google | A 45-minute Googleyness round. | The hiring committee reads interviewer packets, can override panel votes in either direction, including downlevelling, and weighs variance heavily. | C. [S8] |
| Meta | A behavioural round at every level and a leadership assessment before the E6 onsite. | One source says a behavioural round alone can downlevel an E5 to an E4 (the page is partly paywalled). | C. [S5] |
| Microsoft | About one hour mapped to seven competencies; cross-team delivery. | Not stated. | C. [S23, S43] |
| Databricks | Scoping an ambiguous project; handling an outage or model degradation; a culture-fit evaluation. | Hiring-committee review, then a final alignment talk with senior management. | C. [S6] |
| NVIDIA, xAI | NVIDIA: a behavioural and cross-functional round. xAI: pace and ownership under little direction. | NVIDIA: panelists score each round 1-5 and one weak score can sink an offer (a 2024 page, stale). xAI: the founder has historically interviewed senior candidates, and senior loops often add an executive round. | C. [S32, S44, S45] |
| Scale AI, Goldman Sachs, Infosys | Scale AI: working values in a fast-moving company. Goldman Sachs: STAR-style questions in back-to-back sessions. Infosys: project experience and a managerial or HR conversation. | Not stated. | C. [S30, S54, S55, S70] |

K3's Google conflict, stated sharply: the one Google source read says the committee can override a panel in either direction, including downlevelling, and weighs variance heavily; it does not say that a single No Hire sinks the packet at the target level. The wording "sinks the packet" is therefore unsupported as an automatic rule, and the supported statement is that a weak behavioural result can lower the level or the outcome [S8].

### 9. Research loops

| Employer | Stages and formats | Coding and research discussion | Duration and in-person | Grade and sources |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| OpenAI | Application review, recruiter call, a skills-based assessment whose format varies by team, then final interviews of 4-6 hours with 4-6 interviewers over 1-2 days, focused on the candidate's area of expertise; decision about a week after the finals. | Depth discussion of the candidate's own area; coding or take-home depends on the team. | Finals virtual by default, onsite optional; no total elapsed time stated. | A. [S33] |
| Anthropic | Recruiter screen, a 60-minute technical screen, a 4-8 hour take-home for research engineers, one or two ML and research depth rounds, a values round and a hiring-manager round. | Reported themes: walking through a paper, attention variants and tokenisation, scaling laws, debugging a failing training run. The guide describes no research talk or paper presentation. | Not stated. | C. [S29] |
| Google DeepMind | Loop of 5-7 rounds. Research scientist: paper discussion (often 2-3 rounds), research-problem framing, ML coding, maths and theory, behavioural. Research engineer: ML coding, distributed-training design, evaluation-infrastructure design, algorithmic coding. | A 60-minute round implementing a core ML primitive by hand without libraries; probability, linear algebra and optimisation questions. | 6-10 weeks, the research scientist track being the longest; a hiring committee reviews a written packet. | C. [S42] |
| xAI | Recruiter sorting by area, a 60-minute phone screen, a take-home for some senior and research roles, then 4-5 compressed rounds with paper deep-dives, experiment design and training-divergence debugging for research roles. | Comparing architecture choices and reasoning with scaling laws. | Senior candidates may receive offers within 1-2 weeks of first contact. | C. [S32] |

Cognizant's guide is for an AI architect role and its compensation data are in USD, so it is a US-leaning loop and sits in the employer table, not here. Dataford's GenAI-engineer guides rest on 33 reports for Capgemini, 24 for Deloitte, 18 for Infosys and 12 for Cognizant as of 2026-11-12, and its median loop is 3 rounds; the page post-dates the anchor and is used for counts only [S85]. Not found: Indian AI labs (Sarvam AI, Krutrim, Soket AI, Gnani.ai), Indian product companies (Flipkart, Swiggy, PhonePe, Razorpay), GCC-specific pages by name, and aptitude-test vendors' formats (AMCAT, eLitmus, CoCubes, Mettl); see Absences.

### 11. Timelines and rates

Elapsed time from first screen to decision, as reported. Guides describe the intended loop and candidate reports the one experienced; both are kept, and a range is given where they differ.

|---|---|---|---|

| xAI, Perplexity, Cursor | 1-2 weeks for senior candidates; about 3 weeks; 3-5 weeks | Guides | C. [S9, S32, S66] |

Dataset figures (platform data, grade B): Ashby's first-to-last interview span is 14.4 to 17.9 days across technical and business roles, and median time to first fill is 75 days for technical roles against 60 for business roles (requisition-level, not one candidate's wait) [S16]. Gem's average days to hire for engineering is 53 in 2024 against 41 for all roles [S15]; its time to fill for AI role categories is about 120 days or more, again requisition-level [S36]. Gem counts 35-36 interviews held per engineering hire, a funnel count and not the number of rounds one candidate faces [S36]. Rounds per loop are in the loop table; the Dataford median is 3 [S85].

Pass and offer rates. No employer publishes a stage rate. Platform datasets and prep-vendor figures:

| Measure | Value | Population and date | Grade and sources |
|---|---|---|---|
| Onsite-to-offer, engineering | 26% | Gem platform, 2024 | B. [S15] |
| Onsite-to-offer, inbound applicants, all roles | 35% (48% in 2021) | Gem, 2024 | B. [S15] |
| Pre-onsite-to-onsite, inbound applicants, all roles | 20% (29% in 2021) | Gem, 2024; the 29% in circulation for onsite-to-offer matches this 2021 value | B. [S15] |
| Interview-to-offer, engineering | 22% at companies under 500 staff; 14% at 2,000 or more | Gem, data to 2025-05; the page does not define the interview stage | B. [S36] |
| Stage pass-through, all candidates | Recruiter screen about 35%; onsite about 24%; referred candidates: screen 52%, onsite 36%, post-onsite 96%, offer 85% | Ashby, over 54 million applications, 2026-03-31 | B. [S16] |
| Application-to-hire | 0.5% (inbound 0.4%, outbound 2.0%); engineering 0.2%; about one offer per 200 applications | Gem, 2024 and 2025-05 | B. [S15, S36, S86] |
| Applicants per open role | 254 | Greenhouse, 175,000 live jobs, 2026-08-07 | B. [S17] |
| Offer acceptance | Engineering 76% (2024); sourced technical candidates 76% and referred 84% | Gem; Ashby | B. [S15, S16] |
| Onsite loops per hire at new clients | 5 to 15 (an implied 6.7% to 20%) | Karat, page of 2020-11-13 modified 2023-11-10; vendor, COI, background only | C. [S18] |
| Onsite-to-offer ratio across 27 large technology firms | 15% to 70% | Glassdoor reviews (n=15,897), 2019; the author says it overstates | C, stale. [S87] |
| Offer rate stated in a prep-vendor guide | Databricks 20% (n=5); JPMorgan Chase 50% (n=7); Accenture 80% (n=8); McKinsey 20% (n=9) | Dataford guides, 2026-09 | C, tiny samples. [S6, S25, S37, S38] |
| Interviewer disagreement | 38% of paired scorecards differ by at least a point | Ashby | B. [S16] |

Resolution of K6: the figure in the claim does not match the engineering series, which is 26% in 2024, and the 29% appears to be the 2021 inbound pre-onsite-to-onsite value. The industry range of 15-20% is inside the spread of the datasets (14% to 26%) but no single consensus exists. A venture platform team's recommended 80% onsite-to-offer is a target and not an observed rate [S88].

### 13. Practice-prompt coverage

The data appendix holds 90 prompt records with the question theme as candidates report it, what a strong answer covers, red flags and who reported it. Themes are paraphrased; none quotes a source and none is a model answer. The `covers` and `red_flags` fields are practice guidance (grade P); the grade on each record is the grade of the report that carries the theme.

| Track | Prompt records | Employers | Against the 8-15 target |
|---|---|---|---|
| application | 14 | 11 | within 8-15 |
| customer-application | 7 | 4 | below 8 |
| ml-system-design | 7 | 7 | below 8 |
| infrastructure | 9 | 5 | within 8-15 |
| evaluation | 9 | 7 | within 8-15 |
| research | 9 | 4 | within 8-15 |
| governance | 0 | 0 | below 8 |
| product | 6 | 3 | below 8 |
| behavioural | 14 | 10 | within 8-15 |
| coding | 15 | 12 | within 8-15 |

Shortfalls against the 8-15 target: customer-application, ml-system-design and product sit below 8, and governance has none because no interview source was found. Counts for the other tracks come from a spread of employers, but each theme rests on one or two prep-vendor reports. Where an employer's loop was found but no question themes were reported (OpenAI's guide, Deloitte beyond one report, McKinsey beyond two guides), the track coverage rests on the loop record alone.

## Repository Deltas

One row per claim K1-K24. Verdicts use the protocol's words: confirmed (the evidence still supports it), updated (true, but a figure, name or status changed), contradicted (the evidence says otherwise), stale (no evidence newer than the claim's date) and unverifiable (no source either way). Grade is the grade of the evidence behind the verdict.

| K# | Claim (short) | Verdict | Evidence [S#] | Grade |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| K1 | Loops differ by role and team; classify the loop first by asking the recruiter | confirmed | Level differences at Meta [S5]; track differences at Databricks [S6, S7]; an optional domain round at Google [S8]; team-specific assessments at OpenAI [S33]; Cursor's final stage described three ways [S9, S10, S11]. That wrong-track preparation is the most avoidable failure is a judgement no source measures. | C |
| K2 | Classify by team: Google, OpenAI and Databricks vary | updated | Google's fifth round is optional and domain-specific [S8]; Databricks's AI Engineer guide differs from a forward-deployed report and mentions no customer round [S6, S7]; OpenAI's guide says the assessment format varies by team [S33]. Not found: variation by interviewer at Google, and an OpenAI prompt pool mixing classic and AI-product prompts (its guide lists no prompts). | C |
| K3 | A values or behavioural No Hire vetoes at Anthropic, sinks the packet at Google, downlevels or rejects at Meta | updated | Anthropic: a secondary report calls the culture interview the top late-stage gate [S50]; no source read says veto. Google: the committee can downlevel and weighs variance heavily; no source says a single No Hire sinks a packet [S8]. Meta: a behavioural round alone can downlevel an E5 to an E4 [S5]. The only explicit veto found is Amazon's Bar Raiser [S51]. | C |
| K4 | Anthropic's live rounds default to no AI; Meta's AI-enabled round scores directing and auditing; coding or design conflict | updated | Anthropic's default is confirmed [S19]. Meta has an AI-assisted coding round and, in 2026 reports, a separate AI-enabled design round, so the conflict resolves into two rounds [S3, S4, S26]; the pilot is dated October 2025, not spring 2026 [S2, S3]. Scoring covers judgement, review, testing and communication [S3]; Canva calls prompting and accepting output the weakest approach [S21]. No source read uses the phrase No Hire for the round. | A/C |
| K5 | Design-document review rests on three Anthropic reports from April 2026; latency debugging on one | updated | Two PracHub reports were found, 2026-04-16 and 2026-09-18, both from one aggregator [S27, S28]; no latency-debugging report was found and one guide lists no debugging round [S65]. | C |
| K6 | Onsite-to-offer is 29% in Gem's engineering data; 15-20% by consensus; no firm publishes its rate | contradicted | Gem's engineering onsite-to-offer is 26% for 2024 and the 29% matches a 2021 inbound pre-onsite-to-onsite value [S15]; datasets give 14% to 26% [S16, S36]; no employer rate was found [S6]. | B |
| K7 | Entry binds harder than passing; run 3-5 staggered loops | confirmed | 6% of inbound applicants reach pre-onsite against 44% of sourced candidates, and 0.5% of applications end in a hire [S15]; 254 applicants per role [S17]; referred candidates pass the screen at 52% against 35% [S16]. The 3-5 staggered loops tactic has no outcome evidence (P) beyond one anecdote (D) [S23]. | B/P |
| K8 | No prep platform has independent outcome evidence | confirmed | No independent outcome study of an engineering prep platform was found; vendor analyses are self-published [S62, S63]; the one published study found concerns medical-student mock interviews (title only) [S61]. | E |
| K9 | Hello Interview: human mocks ended; Premium 47, 89, 299; ML System Design and AI Coding tracks | updated | Its own page confirms the end of human mocks on 2026-05-31 [S58] and the three prices [S14, S59]; the Premium course list has no ML System Design course [S14]. | A |
| K10 | interviewing.io: calibration praise, cost complaints, GenAI coverage unverified | unverifiable | Its own pricing and any GenAI offering were not retrieved; one Blind thread (poll n=21) gives an unsourced cost complaint [S89]; its analyses are self-published [S62]. | D |
| K11 | Aced is formerly Exponent and absorbed Pramp; free tier plus paid plans | confirmed | The rename appears in Exponent's blog title and a university listing, both title only [S90, S91]; Exponent acquired Pramp's practice-interview assets in 2021 [S92]. The free tier and prices were not verified. | C |

## Open Questions & Risks

The strongest objections an expert would raise, with the evidence found for and against each.

| # | Objection | Evidence for | Evidence against | What would settle it |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| O1 | Loop evidence is prep-vendor marketing and self-selected reports that copy each other and cannot be audited. | Most loop rows are grade C and single-source; PracHub, Dataford and DesignGurus are COI; three Anthropic guides may share Glassdoor and Blind sources [S12, S29, S65]; two ADCI reports may overlap in authorship [S52, S53]. | Meta's AI-assisted round is corroborated by CoderPad's own blog, interviewing.io and two PracHub reports [S2, S3, S4, S26]; Anthropic, OpenAI and Canva policies are primary pages [S19, S21, S33]; Gem and Ashby are large datasets [S15, S16]. | Employer-published guides, or report-level provenance for aggregator entries |
| O2 | Employer guides describe the intended loop and candidate reports the experienced one; mixing them blurs both. | Conflicts on Cursor, Microsoft, Databricks, NVIDIA and Anthropic session length [S6, S7, S9, S10, S12, S43, S44, S45]. | Records keep both readings with sources and flag the conflict; only three employers authored a page read. | Dated, level-specific reports from several candidates per team |
| O3 | Loops change within weeks, so a 90-day freshness class is too slow. | Anthropic's take-home went from 4 to 2 hours in mid-2025 [S20]; Meta's pilot predates the spring 2026 signal [S2]; Google's pilot is planned for H2 2026 [S22]; many guides are undated. | Each row carries an as-of date; the dated sources fall mostly in 2026. | Re-reading the employer pages before an interview |
| O4 | Pass-rate figures mix unlike stages and populations. | Gem's interview stage is undefined [S36]; Ashby's pass-through covers all roles [S16]; Karat's page is dated 2020 and Huyen's data 2019 [S18, S87]. | Each figure is labelled with its population and year, and K6 is corrected on that basis. | A GenAI-specific funnel dataset |
| O5 | Model prices change and vendor pages are marketing pages; context windows are missing. | The OpenAI and Gemini pages show no context-window column; some pages are undated; two Gemini models are previews [S47, S48]. | Prices were read from vendor pages on stated dates; Anthropic's page is the source for Anthropic's own prices [S46]. | Re-reading the pages before every estimate |
| O6 | An Anthropic model reporting on Anthropic's hiring and prices has a conflict of interest. | The author model is Anthropic's, and the policy, take-home and price rows rest on Anthropic's own pages. | Every such row is marked COI; Anthropic's claims about outcomes (that its take-home worked well, and that dozens of the candidates who completed it joined the company) are not relied on; the values round rests on third-party reporting [S50]. | Independent confirmation of the Anthropic rows |
| O7 | Fetch-tool summaries can mis-transcribe numbers. | Pages were read through a tool that returns a summary of the page text. A second read of the Gem report, meant to confirm its figures, returned nothing because a usage limit was reached, and the older Anthropic docs address only redirected to the same pricing page, so no price was compared across two pages. | Every figure names its page and read date, so it can be re-read. The Gem report's inbound stage rates (6%, 20%, 35%, with 84% acceptance) multiply to about 0.35%, close to the report's own 0.4% inbound application-to-hire (own arithmetic, M) [S15]. Meta's AI-assisted round is corroborated across four sources. | Re-checking load-bearing figures on the pages: Gem 26%, Ashby 24%, Greenhouse 254, take-home hours, prices |
| O8 | Coverage gaps limit the conclusions for governance, Indian labs, GCCs and labs beyond four. | See Absences. | Everything missing is listed, and nothing missing is filled from memory. | More sources for those tracks and employers |

## Discovered Concerns

Material findings outside the brief's scope.

- Live-interview copilots carry data and rule risks. Final Round AI's privacy policy permits audio, voice samples and screen captures to be used for AI training with human review and states no retention period, which covers interviewers' voices as well as the candidate's [S60]; using such a tool in a round that bans AI would also breach Anthropic's stated rule [S19].
- Hello Interview's page says the credit refund window for its ended human mock programme has closed, which matters to anyone holding credits [S58, S59].
- Fraud and fake profiles are the stated driver of in-person rounds, but the figures are weak: 6% of candidates admitting interview fraud (n=3,000) and a forecast, not a measurement, that a quarter of candidate profiles will be fake by 2028 [S35].
- The entry stage is under strain: Greenhouse reports 254 applicants per open role and a 412% rise in applications per recruiter with no baseline year [S17]. The brief assigns AI screening to another session.
- Anthropic's account of its own take-home redesign says a model beat most human applicants at the 4-hour version [S20]. That is an employer's claim about its own test (COI), but it implies that other take-homes may be revised, so the lengths in section 5 can change.
- Prep-vendor pages present single-candidate experiences with report counts (33 for Capgemini, 24 for Deloitte) that cannot be audited, and one such page is dated after the brief's anchor [S85].

## Delta

What the evidence did to the priors.

| # | Prior | Status | Evidence | Impact |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| P1 | Anthropic bans AI in live rounds by default; applications and take-homes may allow AI with disclosure | Updated | Live-round default confirmed; AI may refine a self-written application; take-homes default to no AI unless the instructions allow it, and one take-home explicitly allowed AI; the page read states no disclosure rule; an earlier blanket ban was relaxed [S19, S20, S74] | Treat the rule as per stage; drop the disclosure assumption |
| P2 | Meta runs an AI-enabled coding round in a CoderPad with an assistant in 2026 loops, replacing one classic round | Confirmed | A 60-minute round with a built-in assistant; classic rounds stay AI-free; at E6 and above it sits beside a standard coding round; a separate AI-enabled design round is also reported [S2, S3, S4, S5, S26] | Prepare for two AI-enabled round types at Meta |
| P3 | Google reinstated in-person rounds because of AI-assisted cheating | Confirmed | At least one in-person round, announced June 2025, with curbing AI use in virtual interviews as the stated reason [S34] | Plan for in-person rounds at Google; McKinsey asks for a face-to-face meeting too |
| P4 | Take-homes are uncommon at Big Tech and labs and common at AI-native startups; Anthropic uses one for performance engineering | Updated | About 13% of all-role hires include one [S16]; 4-8 hour take-homes are reported for senior and research roles at Scale AI, Perplexity, Cursor, xAI and Anthropic, from guides without counts [S10, S29, S30, S31, S32]; Anthropic's performance-engineering take-home is confirmed and allowed AI [S20] | Expect take-homes at senior and research roles, not at every startup |
| P5 | No employer publishes stage pass rates; aggregates sit at 20-30% | Updated | No employer rate found; datasets give 14% to 26% [S15, S16, S36] | Use 14-26% as the range, not 20-30% |
| P6 | Anthropic, OpenAI and Google list per-million-token prices; flagship input prices run from 1 to 15 USD and output from 5 to 75 USD | Confirmed | Flagship rows: Claude Fable 5.2 at 8 and 40; gpt-6-astra at 10 and 50; gemini-3.1-pro-preview at 2 to 4 and 12 to 18 by prompt length; the legacy Claude Opus 4.1 lists 15 and 75 [S46, S47, S48] | Estimates can use the table, rechecked against the pages |

Rows are numbered S1, S2 and so on in the order of first citation. Access is Read (the page was opened with a fetch tool that returns a summary of its text), Extract (only a search-result title or snippet was seen) or Blocked (a login or paywall stopped the read and was not bypassed). A source that appears under two ledger IDs in the working notes was merged by URL. ## Data Appendix

Records in JSON Lines, one object per line, grouped by type. The `src` field lists ledger numbers (S#). `as_of` is the date the fact describes: the period end or report month that the source gives for the fact where there is one, otherwise the publication date of the newest source behind the record, otherwise the retrieval date where the page shows no date (the ledger marks that page undated). `null` appears once, on the NVIDIA duration figure, because its page gives only the year of the conversations (2024); the `note` says so. `ttl` is the freshness class (V2 for loops, policies and prices; V3 for rates, prompts and practice). Prices are USD per 1M tokens unless the `unit` field says otherwise. In `loop` records the `note` field carries the employer type, region, in-person rule, level and committee facts, the number of independent reports and the source conflicts. In `prompt` records `covers` and `red_flags` are practice guidance (grade P), and `prompt` is a paraphrase of the theme as reported, not a quotation and not an answer.

**loop records (31)**

